- 2026-08-26 — **`yt-script-feedback` skill added: taste and format never share a file, and a rule needs two occurrences plus the owner's sign-off.** New `TASTE.md` holds owner taste rules (numbered, dated, quoting the owner verbatim) and stays separate from the three parsed instruction files (`OUTLINE-INSTRUCTIONS.md`, `SCRIPT-PLAN-INSTRUCTIONS.md`, `SCRIPT-INSTRUCTIONS.md`) — `SCRIPT-PLAN-INSTRUCTIONS.md`'s forms are parsed by `lib/beats.mjs`, and an unrecognised form falls through to plain prose silently, so a preference sitting next to a parsed form invites the next session to "enforce" it by inventing a lane. The promotion threshold is **repeat first, log once**: every reaction gets a `FEEDBACK-LOG.md` row and fixes the current video immediately, but a standing `TASTE.md` rule needs a second item of the same `kind` or the owner explicitly saying "make this a rule" — in the owner's words, *"I don't want to limit the creativity of the script writing, but I want slowly and slowly for this skill to be able to learn my preferences."* The `kind` vocabulary (`hook-length`, `filler-phrase`, `section-order`, `claim-density`, `cta-placement`, `tone`, `pacing`, `jargon`, `structure`, `evidence`, `format`) is deliberately closed, so repeat detection is a string match rather than a fresh judgement call each session — adding a tag needs the owner's approval in the Phase 4 summary, because a new tag resets repeat detection for everything it absorbs. The routing table and the vocabulary live once, in `steps/130-learn-from-feedback-llm/README.md`; the skill reads it at execute time and never restates it, enforced by `test/feedback-surfaces.test.mjs`.

- 2026-08-26 — **Plan 254 moved four taste rules out of `SCRIPT-INSTRUCTIONS.md` into `TASTE.md` as seeded rules T2-T5, and fixed two defects plan 252 left behind.** `SCRIPT-INSTRUCTIONS.md` is a format spec, but its "Voice and tone" section carried four rules that are judgement, not format — one of them ("give every option credit before naming its limit") stated a second time, worded differently, in "Closing convention" item 1. A preference with no recorded origin cannot be dated, sourced, or retired, which is the reason `TASTE.md` exists; leaving these four in the format file defeated that on day one. T2-T5 are **seeded, not folded**: no `FEEDBACK-LOG.md` row and no owner reaction quote, because they predate the log — `From:` cites `SCRIPT-INSTRUCTIONS.md` and the hand-written script it was modelled on (2026-08-11), following `pipelines/video/visuals-flow/TASTE-SIMPLE.md`'s precedent of saying so in the header rather than faking a provenance. The supplied synthesis line ("none of these are bad, they're just built for different priorities") was **deleted rather than migrated** — `TASTE.md` T1 forbids a rule satisfied by pasting one sentence, since that is a template, not taste. `OUTLINE-INSTRUCTIONS.md` was audited and left alone: its by-factor comparison rule reads like taste but is structure, because the scorecard `SCRIPT-PLAN-INSTRUCTIONS.md` builds depends on organising by factor. Separately, plan 252's `steps/110-approve-script-human` "the gate's wording stands" call was wrong — the gate named `script.vo.txt`, which plan 252 itself deleted — and its own done criterion (zero `script.vo.txt` matches repo-wide) was unsatisfiable against its own requirement that step 120's README keep the history that explains why that step was rewritten; both are fixed here (the two stale mentions in step 110 and `.gitignore`/`SKILL.md`, the three correct-history mentions left alone). Plan 252 also rewrote section 1 to forbid a lexicon table in `script.md` but missed section 4's skeleton, which still emitted one — a step-100 session following the skeleton literally would have reintroduced the two-sources-of-pronunciation bug 252 existed to remove; the skeleton's two lines are now deleted. `test/feedback-surfaces.test.mjs` gained a test asserting the four migrated fragments are absent from all three format files and present in `TASTE.md`, and its existing rule-format check was widened to accept a seeded rule's "seeded from … original text" provenance alongside a folded rule's owner quote, since the check previously assumed every rule quotes the owner. **General principle: a rewrite that changes what a file forbids has to sweep every template and example in that file, not only the prose stating the rule — a prose-only test cannot see a skeleton block that contradicts it.**

- 2026-08-26 — **boss and the worktree pool were made Linux/WSL2-capable, and the bug class is the inverse of the usual one: the BSD-first fallback SUCCEEDS on GNU and returns nonsense.** The custody + auto-commit half of this was already done (2026-08-25 entry above), but that audit stopped at the wall and the lander; `boss`, `wt` and `pp-work`'s own age arithmetic were never looked at. Seven sites were wrong, and the interesting one is `stat`. The obvious portable idiom is `stat -f %m "$f" || stat -c %Y "$f"` — BSD first, GNU as fallback — and it is **actively worse than no fallback at all**, because under GNU coreutils `-f` means `--file-system` and `%m` is the mount point: the call SUCCEEDS, prints `/`, and the `||` never fires. Every consumer then did arithmetic on `/`. So `wt`'s lease ages all read zero on Linux (meaning `wt reap` would never free a dead slot, which is precisely the pool-starvation incident of 2026-08-22 re-armed and invisible), `boss-land-sweep`'s lock age was garbage, and `pp-work`'s `ws_touched` fell back wrong. **GNU-first is the only ordering that degrades correctly**, because BSD `stat` has no `-c` at all and simply errors. Same shape in `date`: `date -j -f '%a %b %e %T %Y'` is BSD-only, so `boss_crews_running`'s orphan filter silently stopped filtering on Linux and would report dead crews as live forever. Fixed with three helpers in `boss-lib.sh` (`boss_mtime`, `boss_mtime_fmt`, `boss_date_epoch`) plus standalone copies in `wt` and `pp-work`, which never source boss-lib. Two further blockers: **`BOSS_GH_USER` was hardcoded to the owner's login** and boss *force-switches* the global `gh` account to it on every write path, so a second operator's boss would have hijacked their `gh` session and then failed every call against this private repo — it now resolves `$BOSS_GH_USER` → `git config boss.ghUser` (per-clone, untracked, one command to set) → the original default, so the primary checkout is byte-for-byte unchanged. **That fix shipped a bug worth recording on its own: `git config --get` EXITS 1 when the key is unset, and boss-lib is sourced under `set -e`, so the first draft killed every boss entry script on load in exactly the configuration the change was supposed to leave alone** — the primary checkout, which has no `boss.ghUser`. `test-boss.sh` section (8) caught it because it sources boss-lib in a subshell; `scripts/doctor-portability.sh` did not, because it runs `set -uo pipefail` without `-e`. A `|| true` inside the substitution is the fix, and the general form is: **any `$(...)` in a top-level assignment inside a sourced file is a `set -e` exit for the whole caller, and `git config --get` is the most common non-obvious source of a non-zero exit that nobody reads as a failure.** And **`scripts/node22-path.sh` only ever probed Homebrew prefixes**, so on WSL2 a default Node < 22 produced `FATAL … brew install node@22` — the least actionable message possible on the one platform that has no brew; it now enumerates nvm/fnm/volta version dirs newest-first and prints an `nvm install 22` hint off `uname`. `install-snapshot-timer.sh` is launchd and now says so instead of emitting three `launchctl: command not found` lines and half-writing a plist. **The load-bearing deliverable is section 7 of `scripts/doctor-portability.sh`**, not the fixes: every one of these failures is silent, so the fixes are worthless without a check that notices their absence — it probes `boss_mtime`/`boss_date_epoch` for an actual epoch, the timeout binary, the gh-account/`boss.ghUser` mismatch, the `wt` pool and greenlight, and turns each silence into a printed FAIL. All four suites (`test-wt.sh`, `test-greenlight.sh`, `test-boss.sh`, `test-pp-work.sh`) pass unchanged, and macOS behaviour was re-verified rather than assumed: `boss_mtime` still returns an epoch through the BSD branch, `node22-path.sh` still takes `node -v` from 20.18.3 to 22.14.0, and `BOSS_GH_USER` still resolves to `akshat-git-jpg`. **General principle: a cross-platform fallback chain must be ordered so the wrong branch ERRORS, never so it succeeds with different semantics. `A || B` is only a fallback if A cannot quietly mean something else on B's platform — check that the flag you are guarding on is unrecognised there, not merely differently-documented.** Windows note for a second operator: WSL2 Ubuntu, not Git Bash — the 2026-08-25 work makes the custody chain Git-Bash-viable, but boss's crew management is `ps -o lstart=`, `pgrep -P` and backgrounded PIDs with `disown`, and MSYS PID semantics do not map onto native Windows processes.

- 2026-08-26 — **`pp-work claim` now cuts a new workspace branch from a freshly fetched `origin/main` instead of the main checkout's HEAD, and the reason it went unnoticed for so long is that the damage was never visible in `main`.** `git worktree add -b <branch>` was called with **no start point**, and `pp-work` contained **zero** `git fetch` calls anywhere in the file, so every claim silently inherited however stale local main happened to be. The instinct is to call this a landing bug, and it is not: `pp-land` fetches and rebases onto `origin/main` in its own landing tree (`pp-land:174`, `186-193`), so **nothing stale ever reached main and no commit was ever lost.** The cost is entirely upstream of that — the session *edits* against old code, which buys needless rebase conflicts and, worse, re-fixing a bug main already fixed. The 2026-08-25 Pinterest-archive entry records the exact bite from the other direction ("claim the workspace *after* the prior land completes when both touch the same file"); this removes the need for that discipline. **What made it invisible is `greenlight:481`**, which runs `git merge --ff-only origin/main` on the main checkout after every land: local main is *usually* current, so a claim is *usually* fresh, and the failure only appears when a land happened elsewhere or that best-effort ff did not run — greenlight's own comment at line 478 already warned that a caller "must pull --ff-only rather than assume this succeeded." Three choices in the fix are deliberate. **(1) The fetch is best-effort**: offline warns and falls back to local state, because refusing to open a workspace because a laptop is on a plane is a far worse failure than starting one commit behind. **(2) It fetches only the default branch**, not `fetch origin` — a claim is interactive and on the critical path, whereas `wt get` (which has always done this correctly: fetch, then base on `origin/<default>`, hard-resetting a reused slot) runs unattended. **(3) A re-claim is untouched.** Only the new-branch path takes a base; re-claiming an existing slug resumes that branch exactly where it was left, because rebasing or resetting it could destroy in-progress work, which is the single thing pp-work exists to prevent — there is now a test asserting a re-claim does NOT pick up newer main and does NOT lose an uncommitted file. **`--no-track` is load-bearing and was added after seeing the side effect, not before**: `-b <branch> origin/main` makes git set `origin/main` as the new branch's upstream, which branching from a bare HEAD never did, so without it every `git status` in a workspace would report "ahead of origin/main by N" and a bare `git push` would fail under `push.default=simple`. The local-ahead-wins branch of `claim_base_ref` exists purely for the test fixture: nothing in this repo can commit to local main (the wall blocks the verbs), so "local is strictly ahead" means `test-pp-work.sh`, which commits to a local main and never pushes. **Test 22 was verified to have teeth rather than assumed**: reverting the start point in a throwaway copy makes it fail with "claim did not branch from origin/main", and the suite passes with the fix in place. **General principle: a pipeline that self-corrects at the end will hide a defect at the beginning indefinitely. Because `pp-land` rebases before merging, no output was ever wrong, so nothing pointed at the input being stale — when a later stage is idempotent against a class of error, that error can only be found by inspecting the earlier stage directly, never by checking results.**
- 2026-08-27 — **The main-checkout wall gained a second half: `no-edits-in-main.sh` refuses an Edit/Write to a *tracked* file in the main checkout, alongside the existing git-verb wall.** The existing wall's header explicitly rejects guarding writes, and it is right about the case it names: the 2026-08-22 incident was a correctly-scoped `git add decisions.md` sweeping up a concurrent session's uncommitted 36-line edit, and blocking writes would not have prevented that, because both sessions were entitled to edit that file. **But that argument only covers the commit-sweep failure, not the wrong-tree failure**, and CLAUDE.md already conceded the gap in the same breath as documenting it: "The Stop hook now nags on a dirty main checkout, but by then the work is already in the wrong place." That is exactly what happened today — a session edited a tracked file in main by hand, then hit the git wall four consecutive times (`stash`, `apply`, `apply -R`, and dcg on `checkout --`) trying to undo it. **The wall held perfectly and the work was still in the wrong tree**, which is the tell that the two failures are complementary rather than alternatives: one wall stops the damage, the other stops the misplacement. Three choices are deliberate. **(1) Untracked files stay allowed**, because CLAUDE.md permits "read, talk, and scratch only" on main, a scratch file is untracked by definition, and an untracked file cannot be swept into another session's scoped `git add <file>` — the whole hazard the first wall exists for. **(2) The override is a self-expiring sentinel, not an env var.** `GUARD_OK=1` works for the Bash wall because a command carries its environment; an Edit tool call does not, so the escape hatch is `touch .claude/allow-main-edit`, valid for 10 minutes. A permanent flag file would be a silent disarm, which is the failure mode the first wall's own header warns about (branch-guard.sh being probabilistic on a deterministic invariant). **(3) It fails closed with no JSON runtime**, matching the sibling — a machine in that state already cannot run any Bash command, so refusing loudly costs nothing and passing silently costs everything. It self-identifies its repo by the presence of its own filename under `.claude/hooks/`, so a tracked `settings.json` reaching another machine or another clone matches nothing, and it compares `--git-dir` against `--git-common-dir` in `--path-format=absolute` so a linked worktree is always allowed and a file in `pipelines/`, `apps/` or `tooling/` is still judged correctly (the relative-path trap the sibling's comment already documents). 22 behavioural checks in `test-no-edits-in-main.sh` feed the real hook real PreToolUse JSON and assert the real exit code, including that a linked worktree, another repo, no repo, an untracked file, a not-yet-existing file and an empty payload all pass, and that a backdated sentinel does not. **General principle: when a guard's design note explains why it does not cover something, check whether that reasoning rules the case out or merely leaves it to a different guard. "Guarding writes fixes nothing" was true of the disaster being discussed and false of the one nobody had written down yet.**

- 2026-08-27 — **The session-tag view is now `kbc` (`tooling/cli/pp-agents/`), built on published `claude` commands, and the byte patch (`pp-claude-tags`) is retired — because on 2.1.246 the patch still applied cleanly and stopped doing anything.** The tags feature ships inside Claude Code but its agents view hardcodes the gate to `false`; for three releases a one-byte flip plus a re-sign unlocked it, watched by a launchd agent that re-applied it after every update. On 2.1.246 the tags vanished for the fourth time and the patch reported success anyway. Proof came from rendering the real agents view in a pty on both builds with the same setting and the same patched byte: 2.1.245 drew tag headers, 2.1.246 drew state headers. The binary is a Bun standalone carrying `@bun @bytecode`, so the interpreter executes compiled bytecode and never reads the JS text the patch edits — the patch was cosmetic, and **a watcher that verifies its own edit rather than the behaviour will report green forever**. Pinning the version was rejected outright: the owner wants updates. `kbc` reads `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/jobs/<short>/state.json` and Claude Code's own `group` tag file (so a tag set here shows up there if the feature is ever ungated), and acts only through `claude attach|logs|stop|--bg`. The private daemon sockets (`/tmp/cc-daemon-501/*/control.sock`, `rv/<id>.sock`) were deliberately NOT used — building on those would be *more* fragile than the patch, which is also why the built-in view's `space`-to-reply has no equivalent here. Two things the built-in view cannot do come free: both accounts in one list, and the tag as the primary grouping. See `tooling/cli/pp-agents/README.md`; the forensics stay in `tooling/cli/pp-claude-tags/README.md`.

- 2026-08-27 — **`kbc` reserves `shift+left` to leave a session, and forwards the plain left arrow untouched; `ctrl+o` was released back to Claude Code.** The first build reused the left arrow, which is what the owner already presses: it forwarded the key and then watched the child's output for Claude Code's own agents-view text before detaching. It worked and it could never be quick — Claude Code's list had to finish drawing first, so every exit flashed the wrong screen for about a second. Reserving a key removes the wait and the flash together, and leaves the left arrow free to move the cursor while typing a prompt. Chosen from a menu with the owner: on a MacBook every genuinely free SINGLE key is an F-key needing `fn`, so a one-hand chord next to the arrows beat the alternatives. `ctrl+o` had been a second reserved key and is deliberately gone — Claude Code binds it to "see full summary", so swallowing it took a working shortcut away. `PP_AGENTS_BACK_KEY` adds a second key if the choice ever clashes.

- 2026-08-27 — **A test that gates a land may not depend on wall-clock timing or on live machine state — and when a land parks, read `greenlight.out` before believing the reason.** `kbc`'s suite parked five lands overnight while passing every time it was run by hand. Three distinct causes, all reported by `land.log` as the same "verify failed": a fake session with a fixed 2-second life that a loaded machine outran, so a natural exit read as "the key did not detach"; three heavy suites in one land on a 16 GB machine, where the OS SIGKILLed the last one (`Killed: 9` in the run output, not a FAIL line); and a dropped network, where the tests passed, the merge happened and only the push failed. The fixes are the general rule: assertions now ask which keys the session actually received (`got_key`) rather than how long anything took, and the live-store section copies the real job records into a temp directory and tests the copy. **Widening a timeout only makes a race rarer** — a parallel fix-up agent landed exactly that on the same file the same morning, and it was superseded, though its snapshot idea was kept. `pp-land` names the run-output path in `land.log`; nothing else records why a land really stopped.

- 2026-08-27 — **`kbc`'s `n` asks for a session NAME, dispatches `claude --bg --name <name>` with no prompt, and opens the session; a session waiting for its first prompt is filed as Needs input even though the record says `working`.** The first build asked for a task description and passed it as the prompt, which put the opening turn of a conversation into a one-line field in a list view — the owner's objection was exactly that: *"that description is I think for Claude. I want to have my chat inside Claude only."* It also cost the row its label. Claude Code writes two fields, `intent` (your prompt) and `name` (a summary it generates), and the summary is written **at dispatch time, before there is anything to summarise**, so a one-line prompt gets an invented one: `test1` came back as `onboarding empty state message`, unfindable by the person who typed `test1`. The view worked around that by preferring `intent` while `detail` still read `awaiting task description`, which is a display patch over a dispatch mistake. **The actual fix is a flag that is not in `--help`:** `--name` is undocumented but real, visible in every record's own `respawnFlags`, and it writes `nameSource: "user"`, so the name is never replaced by a later summary — `/rename` inside the session is the same mechanism from the other end. **And `claude --bg` needs no prompt at all**: with a name and nothing else it prints `backgrounded · <id> · <name> (idle — send a prompt to start)` and parks, so the conversation happens in the session's own prompt box with history and editing, which is what was being asked for. Names may contain spaces. Two consequences are deliberate. **(1) The new session opens rather than leaving you on the list** — the name was the only input that flow needed and the session is sitting idle waiting to be talked to, so stopping to make the owner press one more key buys nothing; `shift+left` comes straight back. **(2) `session_state()` overrides the record.** An idle-waiting session records `state: "working"` while doing nothing whatsoever, so the detail is matched on `send a prompt to start` and the row is filed under `blocked`/Needs input. Believing the record would sort a session created five seconds ago into the middle of the Working group, the one place nobody scans for something new. The matcher is the middle of the sentence, not the decorative parentheses, and the test pins the exact real string so a reword breaks the test rather than the behaviour silently. **General principle: when a tool's output looks wrong, check for an undocumented flag before building a display-layer correction. `--name` was discoverable from the tool's own state file — `respawnFlags` records every flag a session was started with — and one flag deleted a whole heuristic, its docstring, and the `awaiting task description` special case that existed only to paper over the wrong dispatch.**

- 2026-08-27 — **`test-pp-agents.py` prints per-section and per-check elapsed time, and the first thing it found was that 380 of the suite's 669 seconds were three checks waiting 60 seconds each for text the program no longer printed. Suite is now 254s for 265 checks.** The owner asked why the suite took so long; there was no way to answer it, because every section is a `section("...")` call and the only signal was a wall of `ok` lines. Bisecting by hand costs more than the instrument, so `section()` times itself and `check()` prints its own elapsed time once it crosses a second. **The expected answer was "the pty tests are inherently slow" and that was wrong.** `drive()` accepts a key as `(bytes, "text to wait for")` and waited up to 60s for that text; three checks named text that had been renamed or never existed, burned the cap **twice each**, and then passed on a *separate* assertion about disk. Two were collateral from renaming the `n` prompt in the same change. The third, `deleting a finished session does not stop anything`, had been **passing for entirely the wrong reason since it was written**: the cursor starts on the first job and the fixture's rows are `[header pp, aaa1 blocked, aaa2 done, header source-filter, …]`, so `j` twice lands on a **group header**, where `d` folds the group and confirms nothing — no delete ran, therefore no `stop` ran, therefore the assertion held. Row order was checked by calling `flatten(group_jobs(...))` directly rather than by reasoning about it. **The fix is the general rule: a missed wait is now a FAIL, not a pause, and the cap is 20s instead of 60s.** A real match lands in well under a second, so the cap only ever bounds the broken case, and a silent timeout is the worst shape that case can take because it is indistinguishable from a loaded machine — the same confusion that made five lands park last night. What remains slow stays slow on purpose: the pure-logic sections are **0.0s each**, and the rest drives the real curses program through a real pty, which is the only way the frozen `n` key, the mouse-reporting leak and the detach behaviour were ever caught. Timing is **printed, never asserted**, because a slow section is a fact about the machine as much as about the code. **General principle: a test harness that can wait for a condition must fail when the condition never arrives. Otherwise a stale expectation degrades into a sleep, the assertion after it covers for the silence, and the only visible symptom is that the suite got slower — which reads as a machine problem and gets a timeout widened instead of a test fixed.**

- 2026-08-28 — **The `codex` executor's default model is `gpt-5.6-terra`, not `gpt-5.6-sol`; `sol` becomes the escalation row.** The Codex CLI's model menu is `gpt-5.6-sol` (frontier agentic coding), `gpt-5.6-terra` (balanced, "everyday work"), `gpt-5.6-luna` (fast/cheap), plus legacy `gpt-5.5`, `gpt-5.4` and `gpt-5.4-mini` reachable only through `-m`. terra takes the default seat because of what a boss plan IS: `orchestrate` only routes to codex when the plan clears the fully-inlined bar, meaning the schemas, snippets and exact commands are already written down and the judgment was spent at authoring time. Frontier reasoning per dispatch buys little against a plan that has no open questions in it, and the ChatGPT weekly limit is the constraint that actually bites — it was at 100% when this was decided. Escalate to `sol` for a large or subtle plan, or as the second round after terra fails once, which is now its own row in `tooling/boss/data/rules.md`. **No code changed to make other models reachable**: `executors/codex.sh` never validated the name, it hands the string straight to `codex exec -m`, so every model in that menu was already usable and an invalid name fails inside the CLI rather than at dispatch. Override order is plan frontmatter `model:` → `boss-dispatch.sh --model` → `CODEX_DEFAULT_MODEL` → `gpt-5.6-terra`, and boss passes `-m` explicitly on every dispatch and resume, so the default only applies to a blank `model:`. The owner's interactive `~/.codex/config.toml` is deliberately left on `sol` — it is machine-local and boss never reads it. Follows the same shape as the agy default (2026-07-18): put the cheap-token executor in the default seat and keep the expensive one as a named exception, rather than paying for headroom no plan asked for.

- 2026-08-28 — **`kbc` remembers which groups are folded, per grouping mode, in the file that already held pins; the "a folded group can hide a Needs input session" gap was named and deliberately left open.** The owner folds the tags he is not working in and every restart opened them again: *"whenever I close the session and when I open the session this again resets and all the tags open, and then I again go back and toggle off the tags."* Nothing new was built for this. `self.pins` was already a set of strings persisted to `~/.cache/pp-agents/pins-<account>.json` through an atomic tmp-plus-rename, and `self.folded` is a set of strings too, so folds ride in the same file: one question ("how was this view left?"), one write, one per-account store that the two commands cannot reach across. `save_view(**changes)` merges rather than overwrites, because two independent things now share a file and a fold must not be able to drop a pin. **The one design decision is that folds are keyed by mode.** A fold name only means something inside one grouping: `pp` is a tag, `Needs input` is a state, and a single flat list would collapse a state group because a tag of the same name had been collapsed — which is exactly why `s` used to call `self.folded.clear()`. It now loads that mode's own set instead, so cycling `s` all the way round returns the view you left. **The cost was priced before building and the owner chose to accept it:** a folded group can hold a session that has gone to Needs input, and the row shows only `▸ pp 5`, not that one of the five wants you. The count in the title bar still includes it, so the number stays honest even when the row is out of sight. Painting a state dot on the folded header would close the gap; it was offered as option A, and option B (persist only) was chosen, so the dot was **not** built — recorded here because the gap is real and the next session should not "fix" it without asking. Two stale facts in the README's "Where state lives" were corrected in passing: it still described `d` as removing the job directory (it runs `claude rm`, since the daemon writes a removed record back) and named `pins.json` rather than the per-account `pins-<account>.json`. **General principle: before adding a store, look for one with the same shape and lifetime. Pins and folds are both "small set of strings, per account, survives a restart, lost harmlessly" — a second file would have doubled the corruption handling, the atomic write and the account keying to hold the same kind of thing.**

- 2026-08-28 — **`kbc` takes the state on screen from the daemon (`claude agents --json`), not from `state.json`, because the record lags by tens of seconds; and the owner was right to disbelieve the first diagnosis.** The report was that a session shows `Needs input` while it is plainly working. The first answer given was that Claude Code writes `blocked` and then stops updating the file, i.e. an upstream bug — **that was wrong**, and the owner rejected it on the correct grounds: *"when I used to have this view in Claude, Claude always used to show me the latest status."* Lived experience beat the measurement, because the measurement was taken badly: an early comparison of `claude agents --json` against the files happened to catch a moment when the two agreed, and that coincidence was treated as proof of a shared source. Sampling it properly showed `71307488  api=working  disk=done` for the session actively running commands. `state.json` is a snapshot the daemon flushes LATER; the built-in view never had the symptom because it asks the daemon. **The fix is therefore not a heuristic.** The first proposal was to infer liveness from the session transcript's mtime (`linkScanPath`, present in all 20 records, 0.1s old while the record was 31s stale) — a sound inference but still an inference, and it was dropped the moment a source of truth turned out to be reachable. `claude agents --json` is a **published** subcommand, so this does not cross the line drawn when this tool was built, which was against the private daemon sockets (`/tmp/cc-daemon-501/*/control.sock`). Two properties were measured rather than assumed, because the owner asked for both: **it costs no tokens** (returns the full list, exit 0, with `HTTPS_PROXY` pointed at a dead port and `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY=bogus` — it reads the local daemon), and **it costs 0.24s of CPU and 190 MB of peak RSS per call**, which on a 16 GB machine that already took an OOM kill this week is the real constraint. So `should_poll()` refuses the call three ways: nothing on screen is `working`/`blocked` so no state CAN change; the keyboard has been quiet for 2 minutes so nobody is reading; or the last call was under 5s ago. A view left open in a background tab makes zero calls. The call runs on a **worker thread** and touches no curses state — a blocking call in the key handler is exactly what froze this view for a day — so a hung `claude` costs one stale tick and nothing else. `live_states()` returns **None** on any failure rather than `{}`, because "the daemon says nothing is running" and "I could not ask" must not be the same value; conflating them would blank every state on screen after one bad call. A session the daemon does not mention keeps its recorded state, since it has usually finished and the file is the only account left, and the idle-waiting override is re-applied on top because the daemon calls a named session with no prompt `working`. `--dump`/`--list` stay file-only: they are one-shot output for scripts and a subprocess per invocation is the wrong trade. **General principle: when a user says "the tool it replaces never had this problem", that is evidence about the data source, not a compliment to the old tool. Two readers disagreeing about the same fact means they are not reading the same thing — find the second source before building a heuristic on top of the first. And a one-shot comparison that shows agreement proves nothing about a lag; sample it while the state is actually changing.**

- 2026-08-28 — **Cloudflare caps cron triggers at 5 PER ACCOUNT on Workers Free, so the tracker's link guard runs on ONE daily trigger that branches on the date, not three triggers.** Plan 258 asked for `["30 0 * * *", "45 0 * * 0", "0 1 1 * *"]` — a daily structural check, a Sunday chain probe, a monthly robot-blocked digest. Deploying it failed twice, for two different reasons, and both are worth remembering. First: `invalid cron string: 45 0 * * 0`. Cloudflare's day-of-week field is **1-7, not 0-6**, so `0` is not Sunday, it is rejected — `7` (or `SUN`) is Sunday. That fix has to change TWO places together, because `scheduled()` dispatches by comparing `event.cron` to the literal string; changing only `wrangler.toml` would leave the handler falling through to `console.warn("unknown cron")` and the pass would silently never run. Second, once the string was legal: `reached the Workers Free limit of 5 cron triggers per account`. `vps-watchdog` (`*/2 * * * *`), `founders-tracker` (`35 18 * * *`) and `gym-app` (`0 4 * * 1`) already hold three, so three more needed six. **Chose the collapse over the $5/mo Workers Paid upgrade** (owner call): one `30 0 * * *` trigger, and `scheduled()` runs the structural pass every day, adds the chain probe when `getUTCDay() === 0`, and adds the digest when `getUTCDate() === 1`. It branches on `event.scheduledTime`, never on `Date.now()`, so a delayed or retried invocation makes the same decision. The Health view's on-screen schedule paragraph was rewritten in the same commit — that copy is visible to the owner and a design change that leaves user-facing text describing the old behaviour is a lie in the product, not a cosmetic mismatch. **The general rule this establishes: Cloudflare Worker crons are a scarce ACCOUNT-WIDE resource (5), VPS crons are free and unlimited, so a new scheduled job defaults to the VPS unless it genuinely needs a Worker's bindings.** The link guard qualifies because it reads D1 and KV through the Worker. Count the account's existing triggers before writing `[triggers]` into any plan.

- 2026-08-28 — **The agrolloo.com WordPress hop was removed from 84 of 119 short links by reading each Pretty Link's destination over plain HTTP, with no access to the WordPress that owns them; and Pretty Links turned out to be the best surviving record of the affiliate URLs, so it also backfilled 27 blank `programs.target_url` rows.** The ask was to cut the third hop (`go.agrolloo.com/<video>/<tool>` → `agrolloo.com/<tool>` → affiliate) and the assumption in the room was that it needed a login to a second Hostinger account. It did not. A Pretty Link is an ordinary 301/302, so `curl -sL -D -` against `https://agrolloo.com/<slug>` reads the destination from outside; 45 distinct slugs resolved in one sweep. **Two details decide whether that sweep is trustworthy.** A browser `User-Agent` is required — with curl's default UA, `agrolloo.com/bookbolt` resolved to a bare `bookbolt.io/` and looked like a dead affiliate code, and with a Chrome UA the same slug returns `bookbolt.io/6671.html`, the correct link. A first diagnosis of "bookbolt is earning nothing" was made and retracted on that basis. And `-I` (HEAD) returns 404 from Pretty Links, so the chain must be read with GET. **The remap is keyed on the current `target_url`, never on `links.tool`**: three agrolloo slugs differ only in case (`Helium10`, `TubeBuddy`, `VidIQ`) and one link row carries a typo'd tool (`submaigc`), so a tool-name match would have silently skipped four rows and left them on the hop. **KV, not D1, is what the redirector serves**, so the pre-flight read all 84 live KV values and refused to build the bulk file unless every one still equalled what D1 recorded; drift was zero, and only then did `wrangler kv bulk put` and the 36 `UPDATE links` statements run. Verification used **HEAD against go.agrolloo.com on purpose** — the redirector logs GET only (2026-08-28), so nine probes against the money path added zero rows to `clicks`, confirmed by the newest click still being a real one from 35 minutes earlier. **Nine links were deliberately left on the hop, and that is the durable part of this entry.** Four are correct as they stand (`langchain` external, `veed` whose programme is REJECTED, `bolt` and `replit` which have no programme) and five cannot be fixed from outside because the Pretty Link itself carries no affiliate code: `filmora` (programme APPROVED on Impact — a real, live revenue loss), `hostinger`, `d-id`, `lumen5`, and `mailchimp`, whose pretty link forwards to a Mailchimp landing page carrying somebody else's Google Ads `gclid`. Only the owner's affiliate dashboards hold those URLs. The nightly guard already reports all nine under `own_redirect_layer`, so the list maintains itself rather than living here. **The hop cannot come back through the UI**: `programs.ts` rejects an agrolloo.com URL on save and `linkhealth.ts` warns on it, both written before this sweep. **General principle: a redirect layer you have lost the credentials to is still fully readable, because redirecting is a public act. Before asking for access to the system that owns a mapping, check whether the mapping is observable from its output — and send a real User-Agent, because an affiliate host that sniffs UA will hand a bot the unattributed URL and make a working link look broken.**

- 2026-08-28 — **All 65 published videos' descriptions were rewritten to `go.agrolloo.com` short links through the YouTube Data API, and the edit itself immediately produced 269 fake clicks, which is why the redirector now filters robot User-Agents.** Before this, 3 of 68 videos sent clicks to the redirector; the other 65 put `agrolloo.com/<tool>` or a raw affiliate URL straight in the description, so their clicks were recorded by Pretty Links in WordPress and never reached the tracker. That is the whole explanation for "57 clicks against 170,839 channel views" — not undercounting, just watching three videos. **Five findings are worth more than the outcome.** (1) **The write account is not the read account.** `videos.list` returns any public video to any token, so reading the channel worked from an account that could not write a byte of it; `videos.update` returned a flat 403 and `channels.list(mine=true)` showed why — none of the five authorised accounts owned the channel. Diagnose write access with a no-op write (send a video its own snippet back), never by a successful read. (2) **`videos.update` REPLACES the `snippet` part rather than patching it**, so a body without `title`/`categoryId`/`tags` blanks them; 48 of these videos carry tags. Every write read the live snippet first and sent it back whole. (3) **All 68 videos carried localizations and 127 localized descriptions held the old link.** Editing only `snippet.description` would have left every Hindi, German, Spanish and Portuguese viewer on the old link — a silent half-failure invisible from Studio's main tab. One video (`7eOwVMuEsbk`) then rejected the localizations payload with `400 invalidVideoMetadata` because its Indonesian entry has **no title** and YouTube will not accept an entry without one; the fallback was to update the snippet alone, after which YouTube propagated the link into all 8 localizations by itself. (4) **Read-after-write on YouTube is lagged.** Verifying 1 second after the write produced a false failure on a video that had in fact updated correctly, and the same lag made a repaired video look like it still had 4 stale localizations. Poll a fresh `videos.list` until it agrees; and never verify from the update RESPONSE, which returns tags in a different order and produced a false mismatch during the write probe. (5) **The bot storm.** Within seconds of each save, crawlers harvested the new URL: 269 GETs in 622 seconds from **143 distinct IPs but only 9 User-Agents**, hitting slugs in exactly the order the descriptions were saved, 195 of them carrying a spoofed `referer: https://www.google.com/` and **not one** from youtube.com. The existing HEAD-only rule missed them because they were GETs, and the `(slug, ip_hash, ua_hash, hour)` dedup missed them because every IP differed. Real clicks went 57 → 326, a 5.7× inflation of the one dataset here that cannot be regenerated. They were deleted and `isRobot()` in `apps/redirector/src/index.ts` now skips logging for self-identifying robots — matching on **User-Agent only**, never on the google.com referer, because a real viewer can arrive from a Google search and a false positive silently loses a real click while an inflated count can always be deleted. The robot is still redirected; only the logging is skipped. 25 tests pin it, including 7 real browser UAs that must still count and a browser-with-google-referer case so nobody "improves" the rule into a referer filter. **The safety pattern that made this survivable, and should be reused for any bulk edit of someone's live content: back up the complete object before the first write; prove the intent with a ROUND-TRIP (apply the change, reverse it, require a byte-identical match with the original) so "only the URLs moved" is demonstrated rather than asserted — 64/64 passed before anything was sent; dry-run everything; do the least valuable item first and verify it by hand; keep a resume log so an interruption cannot double-apply; and audit at the end against the BACKUP rather than against your own expectations, which is the only way a bug in the updater cannot hide.** One unintended change is on the record: `defaultAudioLanguage` went from unset to `en-US` on 29 videos because YouTube populated it itself on write. Benign and arguably correct, not reverted because unsetting it is not reliably supported.

- 2026-08-28 — **The link guard was reporting 58 issues of which ~44 were noise, so the catalogue was aligned to the live links, the affiliate-code rules were widened, and the unmapped-video check now requires a click. It reports 12, all real.** The owner asked for a count of things needing attention in the Health tab. A count is worthless if the alarms are not, so the count was built last and the noise first. **Three separate noise sources, each with a different cause.** (1) **42 of 119 links reported `link_without_program`** because the guard matches `links.tool` against `programs.slug` exactly and 18 catalogue slugs were spelled differently — three of them typos inherited from the sheet import (`submaigc`, `nameheap`, `pipads`) and the rest a hyphenation drift (`invideo-ai` vs `invideoai`, `book-bolt` vs `bookbolt`, `jungle-scout` vs `junglescout`). **The catalogue is the side that moves, never the link**: `links.tool` is baked into short URLs sitting in published YouTube descriptions and can never change. 16 slugs were renamed, two link rows whose `tool` was itself the typo were corrected (`lovable-ai`→`lovable`, `submaigc`→`submagic`), and nine tools that carried live links with no catalogue row at all were added. 42 → 0. **The invariant to preserve: every value in `links.tool` must exist as a `programs.slug`.** (2) **9 of the 10 `no_credit_marker` alarms were real earning links** — `go.expressvpn.com/OryBzA`, `get.brevo.com/538136cj7gdc`, `try.elevenlabs.io/…`, `free-trial.adcreative.ai/…`, `referral.magnific.com/…`, PartnerStack `get.` links, `mailerlite.com/a/…` and the vanity path `vidiq.com/agrollo`. Three rules were added to `linkhealth.ts`: a referral subdomain (`go.`/`get.`/`try.`/`referral.`/`free-trial.`…) **together with** a generated-looking code, a vendor affiliate path prefix (`/a/`, `/r/`, `/l/`), and **the owner's own handles** (`agrollo|khushi|seema|kushal|hvd`) appearing anywhere in the URL — that last one being the most direct possible answer to "will this pay me?". `partners.emergent.sh/`, a bare partner landing page, stays flagged, which is the mirror test. **A subtlety worth keeping:** the first version credited `go.example.com/pricing`, because `/pricing` is seven word-characters and passed a naive shape check. A generated code carries a digit or mixed case; a page name a human typed does not. The rule errs STRICT on purpose — missing an all-lowercase code costs one alarm to dismiss, while crediting a dead homepage loses commission silently. (3) **`unmapped_video` fired for 8 videos that were unpublished drafts.** Minting a short link happens while a video is still being made, so "has links, no YouTube id" describes every draft. Proof they were drafts: zero clicks between them, and none of their short links appeared in any of the channel's 68 published descriptions. The check now takes a `clicks` map and only fires when a code has recorded traffic — a click is the evidence the link is live and its traffic cannot be attributed, which is the actual analytics loss. The 8 draft codes were then deleted outright (12 links, 12 KV keys, 1 click; six of them still have live pipeline cards, so fresh links get minted when those videos publish). **Also fixed while in there: three dead buttons with no `onClick` at all** — `Open all` on the robots card, `Export CSV` (already removed earlier the same day) and, worst, `Retry` on the Health error screen, which shipped permanently `disabled`, so the one control on that screen could never be pressed and the only way out was a page reload. **A general rule this establishes for the Health tab:** the issue groups must PARTITION the list, so the headline count always equals the cards on screen. The previous version filtered against two hardcoded code sets and silently dropped everything else — `own_redirect_layer`, `unmapped_video`, `wrapped_redirect` and `scheme_added` were all counted and rendered by nothing. The third group is now a catch-all by construction, and a test feeds it an invented future code to prove nothing can vanish again.

- 2026-08-28 — **`pp-youtube channel-videos --max N` silently returned only 50 rows for any N, and the account that can READ a channel is not necessarily the one that can WRITE to it.** Two traps found while mapping tracker video codes to real uploads, both of which produce a confidently wrong answer rather than an error. (1) **The pagination bug.** `cmd_channel_videos` made a single `playlistItems().list` call passing `--max` straight into `maxResults`, which the API clamps to 50 per page. On a 68-video channel `--max 300` returned 50 rows, exit 0, no warning — while the CLI's own help text promised "recent uploads (API, any depth)". Any conclusion drawn from that list would have been based on two-thirds of the channel. It now follows `nextPageToken` until `--max` is satisfied. **`description` was added to the returned rows in the same change**, because it is already in the snippet being fetched and it is what lets a caller identify a video EXACTLY — by the `go.agrolloo.com/<code>/<tool>` links in its description — instead of by fuzzy title similarity, which scored zero confident matches against titles like `higgsfiled vs openart vs synthesa`. (2) **Read access is not write access.** `videos.list` returns any public video to any token, so the channel read fine from `kushalbakliwal25@gmail.com`; `videos.update` returned a flat 403. `channels.list(mine=true)` showed why — each of the five authorised accounts owns only its own personal channel, and **Agrollo Reviews (`UCXuXNNuyhtdsiw9bZr0pUxw`) belongs to `adelpaul2526@gmail.com`**, which had never been authorised. **Adding an editor or manager in Studio → Permissions does NOT fix this**: the YouTube Data API ignores channel permissions entirely, so a delegated account's token still 403s. Only the channel's own account, or a Content Owner (CMS) account requiring an MCN agreement, can write. That account was given a **YouTube-scope-only** token via a separate consent (the shared `setup_auth.py` grants nine scopes including Gmail and Drive, which a description editor has no business holding) — the one place in this repo where a token is deliberately narrower than the shared set. **The diagnostic to reuse: prove write access with a no-op write** (send an object its own current state back), never by a successful read. Doing that on the channel's lowest-view video is what surfaced the 403 before a single description had been touched.

## 2026-08-29 — the script desk edits `script-plan.md`, and the rejection it reverses

The local desk now has an **Edit** mode: handles on every note, beat and section
(move, delete, add, edit raw markdown in place), writing the file straight back.

It reverses a note written the previous day — *"the desk is a reader, do not
build editing into it"* — and the reversal is the part worth keeping.

That note came from the owner killing a review-and-markup design: hover tools, a
request composer, an **overlay store** holding comments and edits to be
reconciled into the markdown later. Killing it was right. But his objection was
*"I have the entire thing as a text in my MD file, which I can easily cut paste
everything"*, and it got recorded as "no editing in the browser" when what he
actually rejected was **a second copy of his script**. A day of using it produced
the correction: *"it's not easy to read while editing. I'm not able to follow the
script."*

Edit mode has no second copy. Every control is a line splice on the markdown,
written back atomically, and the file stays the single source of truth. That is
why it is four small files rather than the four plans the rejected design needed.

**The general lesson: a rejection recorded one step wider than the owner stated
it becomes a rule that blocks the right answer later.** Write down what he
objected to, not the category it seemed to belong to.

Guards on the write path (all in `apps/yt-script-desk/server/local.mjs`, none
optional): refuse markdown that will not parse, refuse to overwrite a file that
changed on disk since the page loaded, and back up the last good version to
`.desk-backups/` first. Edit mode is local-only — the hosted Worker serves a
frozen snapshot and the freelancer never rewrites the plan he was sent.

- 2026-08-29 — **53 tracker cards were backfilled with the `video_code` their links already used, by exact title match against `clicks-db.videos`; 13 remain deliberately unmatched because their titles are duplicated.** Only **2 of 76** cards carried a code: `video_code` was added to the card after most links had already been minted, so 66 videos had live `go.agrolloo.com/<code>/<tool>` links whose card did not know its own code. **This was a live trap, not a cosmetic gap.** `/api/link-preview` reads `target.video_code` and, finding it blank, mints a *fresh* code and inserts a new `videos` row — so opening Mint links on any of those 66 would have created a second code and a second set of short links while the YouTube description still pointed at the first, splitting the click history of a dataset that cannot be regenerated. No duplication had happened yet; the duplicate-titled cards and duplicate-titled video rows matched 1:1 in count, which is the evidence that the six repeated titles are genuinely distinct videos rather than accidental re-mints. **The match was validated before it was trusted:** the two cards that already carried a code (`Ixxk`→r0111, `vcfX`→r0116) were included in the title match and asserted to agree, so the method was checked against known-good answers rather than assumed. **The write was `json_set` on `extra_json`, guarded by `json_extract(...) IS NULL`** — merge, never replace, so `topic_date`, `reviewer_email` and `video_tools` survive and the statement is idempotent. One row was applied and read back first (r0027, which carries a `reviewer_email`, so the merge was proven on the hardest shape before the batch of 52). Post-check: 55 codes, all distinct, zero lost `topic_date`, zero invalid JSON, and every card's code cross-checked back to the same title in `clicks-db`. Backup of all 76 `extra_json` values as replayable UPDATEs: `~/kb-scratch/tracker-backups/cards-extra_json-2026-08-29.sql`. **The 13 left alone are `BuIB`, `YYrj`, `csbK`, `e43C`, `eN53`, `gGNS`, `iH6f`, `mICF`, `mMix`, `mjLO`, `ofsf`, `vxTn`, `y4oq`** — six title groups (EverBee, Fliki, Invideo AI ×3, Jenni, Submagic, TradingView) where two or three videos share one title, so a title match cannot say which card owns which code; only click history or upload dates can, and guessing would attach a card to the wrong money path. Those cards remain trapped: minting links on one still creates a second code. **General principle: when a column is added to records that already exist, the backfill is part of shipping it — a half-populated identity column does not read as missing data to the code, it reads as "this one is new", and the code then helpfully creates a duplicate.**

- 2026-08-30 — **The remaining 13 `video_code` gaps were closed from a source that was there the whole time: each card's own upload stage already stored the minted short links, code included, in `card_stages.extra_json.short_links`.** The previous day's entry treated title-matching as the only available signal and left 13 codes stranded across six duplicated titles. It was the wrong search. `short_links` holds the literal `https://go.agrolloo.com/<code>/<tool>` line the description was built from, so the card→code mapping was recorded verbatim on 74 of 76 cards and needed no inference at all. Two other exact keys sat beside it: `card_stages.work_link` carries the YouTube watch URL whose id equals `videos.yt_video_id`, and `extra_json.yt_upload_date` equals `videos.created_at` for every pre-2026 row. **All three agreed on all 13.** The audit that mattered ran backwards over the previous day's work: all 55 title-matched codes were re-derived from `short_links` and **0 disagreed**, which retro-validated the title match rather than leaving it asserted. Post-state: 68 of 76 cards carry a code, all distinct, exactly 1:1 with the 68 rows in `clicks-db.videos` — no live video without a card, no card code without a video. **The pass also corrected a false negative in the earlier entry.** "8 cards genuinely have no links" was wrong; only 2 are (r0114, r0115). Six others — r0105, r0108, r0109, r0110, r0112, r0113 — carry `short_links` for codes `LQfy`, `R1k8`, `27Kd`, `yhpe`, `8rfo`, `O8bg` that exist in **no** `videos` row, **no** `links` row and **no** KV key; probed with a browser UA they all return **404**. They are the residue of an aborted mint: the description text was written to the card but the link was never created. **Those six were deliberately NOT backfilled, and the reason is the inverse of the trap the backfill was fixing:** `/api/link-preview` mints only when `video_code` is blank, so stamping a dead code onto the card would convince the app the video was already done and guarantee it never gets working links. A blank there is a card that will mint correctly; a dead code is a card that silently never will. All six are unpublished (`work_link` is null), so no live description points at a 404. **General principle: before inferring a mapping from fuzzy attributes, grep the record for the mapping itself — a system that renders an id into user-facing text usually stores that text, and the id is sitting inside it. And when a backfill's own null is what triggers the repair path, filling it with a wrong-but-plausible value is worse than leaving it empty.**

- 2026-08-30 — **`keto-kitchen` was fully retired (Worker deleted, source removed, inventories updated) after confirming it cost nothing, and three cards were pruned from the kushal-tools hub.** The 2026-08-25 Pinterest retirement deliberately left `apps/pinterest-landing-pages/` alone on the reasoning that the two Workers sat "on a paid domain"; that reasoning was checked and is only half right. `keto-kitchen.agrolloo.com` is a **subdomain** of `agrolloo.com`, so it carried no registration of its own — Cloudflare lists exactly two zones (`agrolloo.com`, `bridebestie.com`) and the Hostinger registrar holds no keto domain. Its `wrangler.jsonc` declared **zero bindings** (no D1, KV or R2; none of the account's 8 databases or 4 namespaces belonged to it), it never ran on the VPS, and its single 15 KB page fetches nothing but Google Fonts. Total marginal cost: **zero**, which is what made deletion the right call rather than a judgement about the bet. `bridebestie.com` is a real registered zone and was left untouched. **Hub cards removed: URL Shortener, Keto Kitchen, Tutorial VO Studio — but only one of those was a code change.** The VO Studio card had already been deleted from `APPS` on 2026-08-20 in `88142a79`; the live page still showed it because **the hub had not been redeployed since**, which is the durable lesson: `apps/kushal-tools` has no deploy trigger of its own, so an edit to `hub.ts` is invisible until someone runs `npm run deploy` in that folder, and a card can therefore linger on the live grid for weeks while `git grep` swears it is gone. Removing the URL Shortener and Hyperframes-style cards is presentation only — `go.agrolloo.com` is the money path and is completely untouched; only its tile on the private hub is gone. **General principle: "it's on a paid domain" is not the same as "it costs money" — a subdomain of a domain you already own is free, and the question to ask before keeping something alive out of caution is which line item would actually disappear if it were deleted.**

- 2026-08-30 — **The two paid domain registrations now have expiry dates and a decision each, in an INFRA.md table, because "is this costing money" had no single answer anywhere.** Retiring `keto-kitchen` raised the question and the repo could not answer it: nothing recorded which registrations exist, who the registrar is, or when they lapse. `whois` settles it in one line each. **`agrolloo.com` — Hostinger, expires 2026-11-10, renew.** It is the load-bearing one: every deployed app plus `go.agrolloo.com` sits on it, so letting it lapse would break every affiliate short link already published in a YouTube description — links that cannot be edited back out of 65 videos cheaply. Ten weeks of runway as of this entry. **`bridebestie.com` — Cloudflare Registrar, expires 2027-06-02, cancel before it renews** (owner's call this session: a year was paid up front, the wedding/Pinterest bet is abandoned, so use the paid term and drop it). The actionable step there is **turning auto-renew off in the Cloudflare dashboard**, not remembering a date — Cloudflare Registrar renews by default, so a reminder that fires on the expiry day fires after the charge. Everything else is a subdomain and free, which is the fact that made the keto deletion obviously correct. **The table deliberately tells its reader not to trust it** (`whois <domain> | grep -i "Registry Expiry"`) because a hardcoded date silently rots the moment a renewal happens. Mirrored in the memory store as `domain-renewals-agrolloo-bridebestie`, and `context/bets.md` bet 2 now points at the table. **General principle: a cost decision made months before it bites needs a date AND the mechanism that will actually charge you — "cancel before renewal" is not actionable if the money leaves automatically, so the note has to name the switch, not the deadline.**

- 2026-08-30 — **`kbc`'s daemon polling now backs off instead of stopping, and returning from a session counts as activity; the battery-saving rule had switched the feature off in exactly the case it was built for.** Shipped two days earlier with `LIVE_IDLE_STOP=120`: no keypress for two minutes and polling stopped, so an unattended view cost nothing. Both halves of that were wrong. **A keypress is a bad proxy for attention** — watching the list to see when a session finishes involves pressing nothing at all, so the one use that most wants live states was the one that turned them off. And **`shift+left` never reaches the main loop**: it is swallowed inside `run_proxied`, by design, so an hour spent inside a session read as an hour of idleness and you returned to a list that had long since stopped asking the daemon anything. That is precisely the owner's report — *"when I am going back the session still shows as needs input"* — with `tutorial-tracker` reading `daemon=working disk=blocked` at the moment of the complaint, which is the discrepancy the feature exists to hide and which it was no longer polling to find. Fixed by splitting one threshold into three: `LIVE_POLL` 5s while active, `LIVE_IDLE_POLL` 30s after `LIVE_IDLE_AFTER` (2 min) of quiet, and a real stop only after `LIVE_IDLE_STOP`, now **1800s** rather than 120s. An unattended view costs 0.24s of CPU every 30s, under 1% of one core, which is the right price for a list that stays true. `restore_screen()` — the one place both `attach` and `logs` come back through — sets `last_key_at` and zeroes `last_poll_at`, so the tick immediately after you return asks the daemon rather than waiting out an interval. **A second lesson came free: the owner had three `kbc` processes running, started Aug 27, Aug 28 10:51 and Aug 29, and the fix landed Aug 28 14:51 — so two of the three windows were running pre-fix code.** Python reads the script once at process start and `kbc` is a symlink to the repo file, so a landed fix reaches only NEW windows. Worth knowing before diagnosing any future "the fix isn't working" report on this tool: check process start times against the land time first. **General principle: an optimisation that disables a feature under some condition has to be tested against the condition the feature was built for, not just against the cost it was meant to save. "Stop polling when idle" was measured for battery and never once measured for whether the states were still right when the owner looked at them.**
- 2026-08-30 — **There are two Hostinger logins, `pp-hostinger` now takes `--account vps|web`, and the escrow had been silently failing to back up either Hostinger token for as long as it has existed.** The trigger was `GET /api/domains/v1/portfolio/agrolloo.com` answering *"Domain is not registered at Hostinger"* while `whois` named HOSTINGER operations, UAB as the registrar — not a bug, just the wrong account: a Hostinger API token is scoped to one login and reports a domain in the sibling account as if it did not exist. **That failure mode is the durable part** — it looks exactly like "you don't own this", so the skill now says: if a domain you know exists comes back unregistered, retry with `--account web` before concluding anything. `vps` stays the default and plain `API_TOKEN` still resolves to it, so the watchdog, the skill and every prior caller are untouched; the second token is `API_TOKEN_WEB`. **The escrow bug was found by asking where the new token would be backed up.** `infra/escrow/escrow-backup.sh` listed `$REPO/tooling/cli/hostinger/.env` — a path that has never existed, since the CLI reads `tooling/mcp/hostinger/.env`. The script skips missing candidates by design ("a missing entry is skipped, not an error"), so it exited 0 every time while omitting the credential; `infra/secrets/hostinger-vps.env` holds only `ROOT_PASSWORD`, so there was no second copy. Fixed and confirmed with `--dry-run`. **General principle: a backup list of optional paths cannot tell "not present on this machine" from "typo", so every entry that MUST exist needs a positive check — a silent skip is the failure mode that survives longest, because the run keeps passing.** What the web account actually holds is recorded in `INFRA.md`; the load-bearing part is that its Premium Web Hosting still serves the WordPress Pretty Links that 5 `links` rows and 9 published slugs still hop through.

- 2026-08-30 — **An independent Opus review of `kbc` found nine real bugs, two of them shipped BY the live-state work three days earlier, and confirmed the architecture is right. Suite halved to ~125s by removing its last clock.** The owner asked for a second opinion on the whole custom build, not just the reported symptom. Verdict on the shape: keep it. There is **no `claude agents --watch` or streaming mode** (checked, not assumed: `claude agents --help` offers only `--all --json --cwd --model ...`), so polling a published subcommand remains the best source with the private sockets correctly out, and the single ~1900-line file is not past its limit. Two findings were serious and both were made reachable by the polling that landed on 08-28. **(1) The cursor was held by ROW INDEX while rows are ranked by state, so a poll arriving with no keypress re-sorted the list under it — the delete prompt named one session and `y` deleted another.** Held by short id now. Live states turned a latent ordering assumption into a data-loss path, which is the general shape worth remembering: adding a source of asynchronous change makes every "nothing moves between these two lines" assumption load-bearing. **(2) `os.execvpe` in the pty child was unguarded, and it RAISES rather than returning**, so a missing `claude` unwound the exception into the PARENT's code path (`run_attached`'s `except OSError` → `act_open` → `App.loop`) and the forked child became a second curses program fighting for the same terminal. Reproduced with a bogus binary; the trigger is real because Claude Code self-updates. Seven more: a fixed `.tmp` name in `save_view` that two windows interleave into (losing all pins and folds at once); `self.live` never expiring, so after polling stopped the last daemon answer overrode disk records that had moved on; `n` silently not opening a new session when `Ungrouped` was folded, made permanent by sticky folds; `act_logs` missing the `OSError` guard its sibling `act_open` has; bare `ESC` quitting the view on any escape sequence curses could not map; `live_states` using `capture_output=True`, the one call that never got the pipe-EOF treatment the rest of the file exists to document; and `run_claude`'s 45s timeout on the key handler, cut to 12s (measured latency is 0.25-0.6s, and a delete makes two calls, so the old ceiling was a 90-second frozen screen). **`should_poll` was also refusing to poll in the exact case the feature was built for**: it gated on `working`/`blocked` read from DISK, and the disk being wrong about liveness is the premise of the entire path, so booting with every record reading `done` meant never asking. It now also polls while the view is in use and always asks once at startup. **`claude agents --json --all` measures identically to the default** (0.24s, 187 MB) and returns finished sessions, so ending is now as visible as starting. **The README's "under 1% of one core" was wrong** — that is the idle rate; active polling is 5.4% of a core, and three windows make it three times that. Corrected in place. **The suite's `until_quiet(quiet=1.5)` was its last clock**, charged after the first frame and after every key: 0.4s cuts 261s to ~125s with no assertion changes, both verification runs green, one under a concurrent land. **General principle: a review is worth most where the code changed last. Seven of the nine findings were in code under a week old, and the two severe ones were created by the previous fix rather than found in old code — new asynchrony invalidates old invariants silently, because nothing that used to be true announces that it has stopped being true.**
- 2026-08-30 — **There are two Hostinger logins, `pp-hostinger` now takes `--account vps|web`, and the escrow had been silently failing to back up either Hostinger token for as long as it has existed.** The trigger was `GET /api/domains/v1/portfolio/agrolloo.com` answering *"Domain is not registered at Hostinger"* while `whois` named HOSTINGER operations, UAB as the registrar — not a bug, just the wrong account: a Hostinger API token is scoped to one login and reports a domain in the sibling account as if it did not exist. **That failure mode is the durable part** — it looks exactly like "you don't own this", so the skill now says: if a domain you know exists comes back unregistered, retry with `--account web` before concluding anything. `vps` stays the default and plain `API_TOKEN` still resolves to it, so the watchdog, the skill and every prior caller are untouched; the second token is `API_TOKEN_WEB`. **The escrow bug was found by asking where the new token would be backed up.** `infra/escrow/escrow-backup.sh` listed `$REPO/tooling/cli/hostinger/.env` — a path that has never existed, since the CLI reads `tooling/mcp/hostinger/.env`. The script skips missing candidates by design ("a missing entry is skipped, not an error"), so it exited 0 every time while omitting the credential; `infra/secrets/hostinger-vps.env` holds only `ROOT_PASSWORD`, so there was no second copy. Fixed and confirmed with `--dry-run`. **General principle: a backup list of optional paths cannot tell "not present on this machine" from "typo", so every entry that MUST exist needs a positive check — a silent skip is the failure mode that survives longest, because the run keeps passing.** What the web account actually holds is recorded in `INFRA.md`; the load-bearing part is that its Premium Web Hosting still serves the WordPress Pretty Links that 5 `links` rows and 9 published slugs still hop through.

- 2026-08-30 — **The `@agrolloo.com` DKIM records were present in Cloudflare the whole time and orange-clouded, which silently broke DKIM on every outgoing message; and the last 5 short links were lifted off the WordPress hop, so nothing now depends on the second Hostinger account's hosting.** Hostinger's panel warned "some domain records are missing", which reads as *add the records* — the actual state was `hostingermail-a/b._domainkey` existing as CNAMEs with `proxied: true`, so both resolved to Cloudflare's proxy IPs (104.21.68.2) instead of `…dkim.mail.hostinger.com`, and every DKIM verification failed. Flipping both to DNS-only made them return `v=DKIM1;k=rsa;p=…` immediately. **A proxied CNAME is not a CNAME to the outside world** — Cloudflare answers it with its own IPs — so any record whose *value* is the payload (DKIM, domain-verification, ACME, anything a third party dereferences) must be grey-clouded. This one cost ₹0 to fix and was about to be misdiagnosed as "Hostinger has bad deliverability, move to Zoho". **The provider evaluation is worth recording because it inverted:** Zoho's free tier excludes IMAP/POP/ActiveSync (webmail only) and caps at 5 users, against Hostinger's free tier at 100 mailboxes with full client access — so the "cheap upgrade" was a downgrade on every axis except storage, and would not have fixed deliverability, because the fault was in DNS, not the mail host. **Then the hop:** `CLICKS_KV` + `links` for `5MyF/filmora`, `L2Is/lumen5`, `SB2g/hostinger`, `f5g4/d-id`, `zhaY/mailchimp` were repointed straight at their vendors, taking `links` rows that depend on `agrolloo.com` from 5 to **0**, all verified live. No YouTube edit was needed — descriptions already point at `go.agrolloo.com`, which is the entire return on having put a shortener in the middle. `zhaY/mailchimp` had been sending clicks to a Mailchimp lander carrying **someone else's Google Ads `gclid`**, crediting a stranger's ad spend; it now goes to the plain homepage. All five still earn ₹0 for want of an affiliate code, `filmora` being the one with an approved Impact programme behind it. **The Hostinger API has no email endpoints whatsoever** (nine paths, all 404) — domains, VPS, hosting, DNS and billing only — so mailbox questions genuinely require the panel; but `pp-hostinger --account web dns list <domain>` returns Hostinger's *intended* zone even when DNS is delegated elsewhere, which is how the DKIM targets were recovered without touching the panel.
- 2026-08-30 — **There are two Hostinger logins, `pp-hostinger` now takes `--account vps|web`, and the escrow had been silently failing to back up either Hostinger token for as long as it has existed.** The trigger was `GET /api/domains/v1/portfolio/agrolloo.com` answering *"Domain is not registered at Hostinger"* while `whois` named HOSTINGER operations, UAB as the registrar — not a bug, just the wrong account: a Hostinger API token is scoped to one login and reports a domain in the sibling account as if it did not exist. **That failure mode is the durable part** — it looks exactly like "you don't own this", so the skill now says: if a domain you know exists comes back unregistered, retry with `--account web` before concluding anything. `vps` stays the default and plain `API_TOKEN` still resolves to it, so the watchdog, the skill and every prior caller are untouched; the second token is `API_TOKEN_WEB`. **The escrow bug was found by asking where the new token would be backed up.** `infra/escrow/escrow-backup.sh` listed `$REPO/tooling/cli/hostinger/.env` — a path that has never existed, since the CLI reads `tooling/mcp/hostinger/.env`. The script skips missing candidates by design ("a missing entry is skipped, not an error"), so it exited 0 every time while omitting the credential; `infra/secrets/hostinger-vps.env` holds only `ROOT_PASSWORD`, so there was no second copy. Fixed and confirmed with `--dry-run`. **General principle: a backup list of optional paths cannot tell "not present on this machine" from "typo", so every entry that MUST exist needs a positive check — a silent skip is the failure mode that survives longest, because the run keeps passing.** What the web account actually holds is recorded in `INFRA.md`; the load-bearing part is that its Premium Web Hosting still serves the WordPress Pretty Links that 5 `links` rows and 9 published slugs still hop through.

- 2026-08-30 — **The `@agrolloo.com` DKIM records were present in Cloudflare the whole time and orange-clouded, which silently broke DKIM on every outgoing message; and the last 5 short links were lifted off the WordPress hop, so nothing now depends on the second Hostinger account's hosting.** Hostinger's panel warned "some domain records are missing", which reads as *add the records* — the actual state was `hostingermail-a/b._domainkey` existing as CNAMEs with `proxied: true`, so both resolved to Cloudflare's proxy IPs (104.21.68.2) instead of `…dkim.mail.hostinger.com`, and every DKIM verification failed. Flipping both to DNS-only made them return `v=DKIM1;k=rsa;p=…` immediately. **A proxied CNAME is not a CNAME to the outside world** — Cloudflare answers it with its own IPs — so any record whose *value* is the payload (DKIM, domain-verification, ACME, anything a third party dereferences) must be grey-clouded. This one cost ₹0 to fix and was about to be misdiagnosed as "Hostinger has bad deliverability, move to Zoho". **The provider evaluation is worth recording because it inverted:** Zoho's free tier excludes IMAP/POP/ActiveSync (webmail only) and caps at 5 users, against Hostinger's free tier at 100 mailboxes with full client access — so the "cheap upgrade" was a downgrade on every axis except storage, and would not have fixed deliverability, because the fault was in DNS, not the mail host. **Then the hop:** `CLICKS_KV` + `links` for `5MyF/filmora`, `L2Is/lumen5`, `SB2g/hostinger`, `f5g4/d-id`, `zhaY/mailchimp` were repointed straight at their vendors, taking `links` rows that depend on `agrolloo.com` from 5 to **0**, all verified live. No YouTube edit was needed — descriptions already point at `go.agrolloo.com`, which is the entire return on having put a shortener in the middle. `zhaY/mailchimp` had been sending clicks to a Mailchimp lander carrying **someone else's Google Ads `gclid`**, crediting a stranger's ad spend; it now goes to the plain homepage. All five still earn ₹0 for want of an affiliate code, `filmora` being the one with an approved Impact programme behind it. **The Hostinger API has no email endpoints whatsoever** (nine paths, all 404) — domains, VPS, hosting, DNS and billing only — so mailbox questions genuinely require the panel; but `pp-hostinger --account web dns list <domain>` returns Hostinger's *intended* zone even when DNS is delegated elsewhere, which is how the DKIM targets were recovered without touching the panel.
